Never improvise one.** A guessed clearance that happens to\nbuild is worse than a failure, because it prints. Record what you are told in\n`measurements.toml` at the project root, with how it was obtained, and read it with\n`measured()`:\n\n```toml\n[shelf_depth]\nvalue = 340\n320→unit = \"mm\"\nhow = \"tape measure, freezer middle shelf, 2026-07-20\"\n```\n\n```python\nfrom nurb import measured\n\ndepth = measured(\"shelf_depth\")\n```\n\n330→An unknown name raises, naming what is on file. That failure is the point: it is the\nmoment to ask rather than to pick something plausible.\n\n**A published number is a measurement, not a guess.** When the part mates with a manufactured product or a published standard, a VESA mount, a gridfinity bin, a camera thread, the number already exists in a datasheet, and making somebody caliper a hole pattern a standards body fixed is friction wearing rigor's clothes. Research it, record it like any other measurement with `how` naming the source (\"VESA MIS-D 100, manufacturer product page\"), and ask only for what nobody published, like the opening the finished thing has to fit. Two limits keep this honest: the user's actual object outranks the spec when the two disagree, because clones drift, and a number inferred from a product photo or a listing's marketing copy was never published at all, so it is a guess and gets marked like one.\n\n**A photo is a shape, never a dimension.** It can identify the standard, that the siding is dutch lap, that the rail is 2020 extrusion, and the published profile carries the numbers from there. Pixels carry no millimetres, so a dimension read off a photo is a guess and gets marked like one.\n\n**A phone scan is reference geometry, not metrology.** Its error changes with the phone, capture mode, surface and technique, so no universal millimetre threshold makes it a caliper. `nurb scan` reads the mesh in mm, states the units it used, and slices profile polylines to sketch against. Record what it gave with `how` naming the file and the slice, and keep every scan-derived fit `provisional` until a coupon proves it against the real object.\n\n**A fit coupon turns a loose measurement into a tight one.** Before printing a part modelled against a scan or a photo, print the mating surface alone: a thin strip carrying just the profile, minutes of filament. Held against the real object it shows every gap the numbers hid, the parameters get corrected, and only then does the full part print. A coupon that fits is what takes the provisional flag off.\n340→\n**When there is nobody to ask**, write the guess down and mark it `provisional = true`.\nThe danger was never the guess, it is that a guess and a measurement look identical six\nmonths later. `how` is still required, because \"eyeballed against a broom\" tells the\nnext person how far to trust it, and `nurb check` lists every provisional value until\nsomebody picks up a caliper.\n\nA measured value pays for itself across a family. Notch measured one bracket pitch and\namortized it over sixteen parts.\n\n350→## Assemblies\n\nEvery rule above judges one solid being manufactured. An assembly judges solids being\nused together: a door on its mount, a lid over the machine it covers. The failure it\nexists for built clean, checked clean, exported watertight and printed beautifully,\nthen jammed at 45 degrees of its swing, because the collision between two correct\nparts existed only in the physical world. No per-solid rule can have an opinion about\nmotion.\n\nThe contract mirrors a part's. An assembly is a function in `parts/` whose keyword\n360→defaults are its parameters; what it returns is placed solids rather than one solid.\n`use(name)` builds a sibling part where it was modelled, `hinge(solid, axis, through,\nat)` declares a revolute joint and poses it, and `obstacle(solid, name)` adds what the\nparts mount into: the machine, the wall, the shelf above. The joint angle is an\nordinary keyword default, so the viewer's slider swings it live, and the slider ends\nexactly where the declared range does.\n\n**The declared range is a claim, and `nurb check` audits it** the way min_wall audits\na wall: each hinge sweeps through it against everything else in the scene, and a\nfinding is the angle where it jams plus the coordinates of the contact. Declare what\n370→the design needs, never what happens to pass. A door that must stay open by gravity\nneeds past 90 degrees; declaring (0, 90) because that sweeps clean is writing the test\nto match the bug.\n\n**Nothing full width may cross the pivot line.** The mount that carries a hinge has to\nreach the pin from somewhere, so some of it always lives above or behind the pin, and\nany full-width material on the moving part that reaches past the pivot sweeps straight\ninto it. The door that jammed at 45 did so against its own top edge, carried past the\npin to support the knuckles; the knuckles got narrow tabs instead, sized to pass\nbeside the mount, and the panel now starts at the pivot line.\n380→\n**Obstacles are boxes and prisms, never thin hand-drawn profiles.** Three nearly\ncollinear points make a razor sliver, and OCCT answers a boolean against a degenerate\nsolid with chunks of the other operand: measured once as a 152,000mm3 phantom\ncollision at a pose that was actually clear. The sweep refuses any intersection that\nis not a subset of its inputs and names the solid to fix, but the fix is to model the\nobstacle as something with honest volume in the first place.\n\n**Tangent is clear, and clear is not clearance.** Collision is intersection volume, so\ntwo faces that kiss at zero volume pass, the closed door resting on its stop included.\n390→In plastic a zero-clearance pass is already a bind. The declared range should carry\nthe same honesty about clearance that a tongue's `fit` carries about width.\n\n**The stop the sweep finds can be the detent the design wants.** The door that\nmotivated all of this rests open at 240 degrees against the back of its own mount,\nheld there by gravity, and that contact is a feature with a card entry, not a finding\nto engineer away. Read a jam before redesigning around it.\n\n**Never trust a swing worked out by hand.** The same hinge was hand-modelled three\ntimes and gave three answers, one of them \"impossible\"; the wrong one ran a convex\n400→test against a non-convex profile, which is exactly the kind of quiet modelling error\na kernel boolean cannot make. The sweep is the authority, and it is cheap.\n\nOne hinge sweeps at a time; the others hold their pose, which is the conservative\nreading of a mechanism you can only move one hand at a time. Printability rules stay\noff assemblies entirely: each part already answered them alone, and overhang measured\non an assembled scene reports confident nonsense.\n\n## Verification\n\n410→\"It built\" is not verification. `nurb verify` runs the machine-checkable part of\nthis list: one solid per configuration, every count flexed upward, the rules clean,\nand the card agreeing with the geometry. `--report` writes the verdict into\n`build/renders/<part>.verify.md` with the renders that back it beside it, one still\nper finding. The two it cannot do are the two that need you, and they are items 2 and 6.\n\nBefore presenting a part:\n\n1. **Flex the driving parameters up as well as down**, for example 4 to 6 to 2 to 4.\n   Growth is what catches a frozen selector; shrinking alone passes a broken part.\n420→2. **Check fit-critical faces by coordinate** after any polish or resize. `nurb inspect`\n   lists them, with each finding resolved to the face it fired on. Which faces are\n   fit-critical is still yours to know, which is why this is not something `nurb verify`\n   can run for you.\n3. **Confirm the solid count**, which is almost always one.\n4. **Run `nurb check`.** Zero findings is the bar, and a finding fired at a part that\n   prints fine is a bug in the rule, not something to accept quietly.\n5. **Predict a baseline before you look at it.** Working out what the sliver count\n   should be from the polish exclusions turns the number into a test of the rule instead\n   of something to write down.\n430→6. **Render it and actually look.** `nurb render <part>` writes a PNG, `--section\n   z:4mm` cuts it open where outside views cannot reach, and `nurb inspect --render`\n   stands a camera at every finding with the guilty face painted. A part can pass\n   every numeric check and still be visibly wrong.\n\n===== API =====\n  from nurb import *  gives a part file all of build123d, plus:\n\n  part(fn)\n      Mark a function as a part.\n440→  reject(message, param=None)\n      Refuse to build a configuration the part knows cannot work.\n  is_convex(edge, f1, f2)\n      True if the material folds away from itself across this edge.\n  concave_edges(shape)\n      Every concave edge, which is where polish is forbidden and stress collects.\n  measured(name, start=None)\n      A measured value, in mm.\n  polish(shape, edges, size)\n      Chamfer as much of `edges` as will land, and stop there.","absolute_path":"/private/var/folders/s4/by90nw7j7_l_nvvqs3hd9mjh0000gn/T/nurb-grok-home-_935y9ir/sessions/%2Fprivate%2Fvar%2Ffolders%2Fs4%2Fby90nw7j7_l_nvvqs3hd9mjh0000gn%2FT%2Fnurb-eval-trial-gftno7aq%2Fproject/01a03962-58e4-75e1-8c8d-52b69509187b/terminal/call-8d2c9299-4b9d-4172-92b5-038b2e80e0e4-3.log","offset":200,"limit":250,"raw_output":"**Sliver check after every polish pass.** Faces under 1mm² are a polish bug unless\njustified. The only ones the doctrine allows outright are the corner triangles where\nthree 1mm chamfers meet at a convex corner, about 0.87mm². Anything else is usually\nchamfers colliding near a mating mouth, or a chamfered concave edge. A part declares the\ncount it has earned on its card, so a new one is a regression and a known one is silent.\n\n## Kernel rules\n\nSpecific to OCCT, and measured on real parts rather than reasoned about.\n\n- **Two chamfered convex edges need more than `2 * chamfer_size` of face between them.**\n  Closer and OCCT raises `BRep_API: command not done`, which build123d surfaces as\n  \"Failed creating a chamfer, try a smaller length value(s)\". At 1mm the shelf fails\n  with 1.66mm between the edges and builds with 2.16mm; at 0.5mm it builds with 1.16mm.\n  The threshold tracks the chamfer exactly. This is the single most common way a part\n  stops building, so `chamfer` says this much at the point of failure rather than\n  leaving the kernel's advice to stand: taking \"try a smaller length\" at its word is how\n  a part ends up with a 0.4mm chamfer that lands and then prints as a defect.\n- **One chamfered edge needs more than `chamfer_size` of face**, which is the same rule\n  with one chamfer instead of two and is easy to miss because it looks like plenty of\n  room. It bites wherever a polished edge sits beside something that is never polished:\n  a concave junction, a structural chamfer's toe, a pocket wall. Measured on three\n  different parts, all at the same threshold: 1.08mm builds and 1.00mm fails at a 1mm\n  chamfer.\n- **A batch chamfer failure is not a bad edge.** Every edge in a failing set chamfers\n  fine on its own, so testing them one at a time reports that nothing is wrong. Bisect\n  the set pairwise instead. \"Try a smaller length\" would never have found the rule above.\n- **The second way a chamfer dies, where bisecting does not help**, is an edge whose end\n  lands on a vertex with four faces around it and only three edges between them. Two of\n  those faces touch at a point without sharing an edge, and OCCT has no cap for that\n  corner. The edge fails on its own, on a clean body, at every length down to 0.05mm, so\n  nothing about the failure looks like a clearance problem. The fix is to chamfer in two\n  passes so that the first one gives those two faces a real edge to meet along, then\n  reselect from the result. Found on the parts bin, where the front drop and the side\n  taper land at the same height by design.\n- **Prefer `new_edges` over geometric selectors.** Each chamfer changes topology, so a\n  selector resolved against pristine geometry drifts once an earlier chamfer runs.\n  `new_edges(before, combined=after)` returns exactly the edges an operation created. It\n  is the algebra-mode equivalent of a builder's `Select.LAST`, and algebra mode is what a\n  part file uses, so `part.edges() - last` is not available. It is what makes a part\n  survive flexing its counts.\n- **A Fusion workaround is sometimes a real constraint wearing a disguise.** The gusset\n  drop that existed only to escape `ASM_BL_NO_MATE` turned out to be the same \"a chamfer\n  needs room to land\" constraint at a different number. Deleting it would have been\n  wrong; restating it as a rule was right.\n\n## Cards\n\nA part file carries geometry, not rationale. Every part worth keeping has a card next to\nit, same basename. **Read the card before editing the part, and update it when done.**\n\nFour sections, and one of them does work nothing else does:\n\n- `## What it is`\n- `## Design notes`, why the key dimensions are what they are\n- `## Don't`, **required**, what was tried and rejected. Geometry records what is; only\n  this records what is deliberately absent. Without it an agent helpfully re-adds the\n  lead-in chamfer that was retired on purpose.\n- `## Changelog`, dated\n\nPlus two machine-facing pieces:\n\n- The **AUTO block**, regenerated by `nurb card`. Never hand-edit it. It is a cache, not\n  a source of truth, and it holds no timestamp so regenerating it on unchanged geometry\n  produces no diff.\n- The **accepted baselines**, a TOML fence carrying what this part has already justified:\n  its sliver count, its real minimum wall, its printer settings. The number goes next to\n  the sentence that earns it, because a count on its own is a magic number. Machine facts\n  stay out of it: a bed size belongs to the machine, so it lives in `printer.toml` at the\n  project root, which names a shipped profile (`profile = \"bambu_a1_mini\"`) and can\n  override any check setting machine-wide. A printer is really a fact about the workshop,\n  not the project, so `~/.config/nurb/config.toml` takes the same schema and covers every\n  project on the machine; `printer.toml` overrides it where they disagree.\n\n```toml\n[part]\nmin_wall = 1.0\nforward = [-1, 0, 0]\n\n[accepted]\nsliver = 6\n```\n\n### Variants\n\nA catalog entry that is the same function at different numbers is a variant, not a new\nfile. It goes in the same fence, and `build`, `check`, `card` and `export` all walk it\nexactly as they walk the part's own defaults, so it gets its own STL, its own baselines\nand its own line in the AUTO block.\n\n```toml\n[variants.shelf_gridfinity_3x2]\nnote = \"The same shelf sized for the wide bin: three columns instead of two.\"\n\n[variants.shelf_gridfinity_3x2.params]\ngrid_x = 3\nbracket_count = 6\n\n[variants.shelf_gridfinity_3x2.accepted]\nsliver = 26\n```\n\nThe test is whether it is the same geometry. A wider cradle on the same J is a variant.\nA different mechanism is a part, however similar the slab looks.\n\n`note` is one sentence saying why the variant exists, written in the user's words rather than the doctrine's, because the viewer shows it next to the variant and its reader is whoever is about to print the thing. An agent cannot\nmeasure a freezer shelf, and a guessed number produces a perfect model of the wrong\nobject: it builds, it checks clean, and it prints. Nothing downstream catches it.\n\nSo a measurement is a named value on disk with a note saying how it was obtained, and\nasking for one that is not on file raises. That failure is the feature. Without\n    that stop it would keep going to the filesystem root and could silently answer with\n    some unrelated file from an ancestor directory, which is the exact failure this whole\n40→    module exists to prevent: a dimension that is wrong and looks fine.\n    \"\"\"\n    for d in [start, *start.parents]:\n        if (d / FILENAME).is_file():\n            return d / FILENAME\n        if (d / \"parts\").is_dir():\n            return None\n    return None\n\n\n50→def _read(path):\n    \"\"\"Parse it, and say which file when it will not parse. The failure mode is not putting a number in, it is that the number then\n70→    looks exactly like one somebody measured. Six months later nothing distinguishes\n    them and the part is wrong for a reason no one can find.\n\n    So a guess is allowed to be written down and required to say so, and `nurb check`\n    reports the ones that still are. `how` stays mandatory either way: a provisional\n    value still has to say where it came from, because \"eyeballed against a broom\"\n    tells the next person how much to trust it.\n    \"\"\"\n    return sorted(\n        (name, entry.get(\"how\", \"\"))\n80→        for name, entry in load(start).items()\n        if entry.get(\"provisional\")\n    )\n\n\ndef measured(name, start=None):\n    \"\"\"A measured value, in mm.\n\n    Raises when it is not on file, when it has no provenance, or when it is recorded in\n    units this does not work in. All three are cases where continuing would produce\n90→    geometry that looks right and is wrong.\n\n    A value marked `provisional = true` is returned like any other. It is a real number\n    that builds a real part; what it is not is measured, and `nurb check` says so until\n    somebody picks up a caliper.\n    \"\"\"\n    start = pathlib.Path(start).resolve() if start else _caller_dir(2)\n    path = _find(start)\n    if path is None:\n        raise MeasurementError(\n100→            f\"no {FILENAME} above {start}, so there is nothing measured to ask for. \"\n            f\"Measure {name!r}, then record it:\\n\\n\"\n            f'  [{name}]\\n  value = 0\\n  unit = \"mm\"\\n  how = \"how you measured it\"\\n\\n'\n            f\"If there is nobody to ask right now, add `provisional = true` and put \"\n            f\"your best guess in. `nurb check` will keep reminding you.\"\n        )\n    book = _read(path)\n    if name not in book:\n        have = \", \".join(sorted(book)) or \"nothing yet\"\n        raise MeasurementError(\n110→            f\"nothing measured called {name!r} in {path.name}. have: {have}. \"\n            f\"Ask for the measurement rather than guessing one: a wrong number here \"\n            f\"builds, checks clean, and prints.\"\n        )\n    entry = book[name]\n    if \"value\" not in entry:\n        raise MeasurementError(f\"{path.name}: {name} has no value\")\n    if \"how\" not in entry:\n        raise MeasurementError(\n            f\"{path.name}: {name} does not say how it was measured. {"type":"tool_call_update","toolCallId":"call-31e535db-87ec-46e6-85c5-a077fbbaa85e-16","status":"completed","content":[{"type":"content","content":{"type":"text","text":"1→\"\"\"The polish pass: chamfer as much of a set as the kernel will take.\n\n`chamfer` is all or nothing. One edge that cannot land takes the whole call down, and\nthe natural response is to shrink the set by hand until it builds. That is how a part\nwith ninety exposed edges ends up with three chamfered ones, and it is why the same\ndoctrine produces far more chamfers in a GUI, where a human adds them one feature at a\ntime and works around each collision individually.\n\nThe doctrine describes this algorithm and used to leave every project to write it. It\n10→was 35 lines in one `system.py`, which meant the next project would write it again or,\nfar more likely, skip it and hand-narrow the set, which is exactly what the rule exists\nto prevent.\n\"\"\"\n\nimport collections.abc\nimport functools\n\nfrom build123d import chamfer as _chamfer\n\n20→# What OCCT says when a chamfer will not land. The same strings `nurb verify`\n# matches on, because both are asking the same question: did the kernel refuse, or did\n# the part refuse in its own words.\nKERNEL = (\"Failed creating a chamfer\", \"BRep_API\")\n\n\ndef _advice(count, size, word):\n    \"\"\"What the doctrine knows about this failure, said at the moment it happens.\n\n    The kernel's own message is \"try a smaller length value(s)\", and the doctrine is\n30→    explicit that following it would never have found the real rule: a chamfer fails\n    for room, not for length. Shrinking it is the one response that makes a part quietly\n    worse, because a 0.4mm chamfer lands and then reads as a defect on the print.\n\n    So the rule goes here rather than only in `nurb rules`. This is the most common way\n    a part stops building, and an error is the one place a reader is guaranteed to be\n    looking.\n    \"\"\"\n    lines = [\n        \"\",\n40→        f\"  A {word} fails for room, not for length, and shrinking it is usually the\",\n        f\"  wrong fix. Two {word}ed convex edges need more than {2 * size:g}mm of face between\",\n        f\"  them, and a single one needs more than {size:g}mm.\",\n    ]\n    if count > 1:\n        lines += [\n            \"\",\n            f\"  This call passed {count} edges as one batch, and a batch is all or nothing:\",\n            \"  one edge that cannot land takes the whole call down. Every edge in it very\",\n            \"  likely chamfers fine on its own, so testing them one at a time will report\",\n50→            \"  that nothing is wrong.\",\n            \"\",\n            # `!r` not `:g`: a size is a measurement, and this codebase reads a bare `1`\n            # as a count. Suggested code has to echo back the float the caller passed.\n            f\"  polish(shape, edges, {size!r}) bisects the set for you and keeps the halves that\",\n            \"  build, which is what the doctrine prescribes instead of narrowing by hand.\",\n        ]\n    else:\n        lines += [\n            \"\",\n60→            \"  A single edge that fails at every length is the other case: its end lands on\",\n            \"  a vertex with four faces and only three edges between them, and OCCT has no\",\n            \"  cap for that corner. Chamfer in two passes and reselect from the result.\",\n        ]\n    lines += [\"\", \"  `nurb rules` has both cases in full.\"]\n    return \"\\n\".join(lines)\n\n\ndef _count(objects):\n    try:\n70→        return len(list(objects))\n    except TypeError:  # a single Edge or Vertex, which is not iterable\n        return 1\n\n\ndef _guarded(fn, word, objects, size, *args, **kwargs):\n    # A generator would be exhausted by the failing call, so the count in the message\n    # would read zero and the advice would describe the wrong case. Lists, ShapeLists\n    # and a single Edge are all left alone.\n    if isinstance(objects, collections.abc.Iterator):\n80→        objects = list(objects)\n    try:\n        return fn(objects, size, *args, **kwargs)\n    except Exception as exc:\n        if not any(k in str(exc) for k in KERNEL):\n            raise\n        # Same type and same opening line, so `nurb verify`'s kernel matching and any\n        # caller catching ValueError still work. `from None` because the original adds\n        # nothing a reader wants: it is the same sentence again under a C++ frame.\n        raise type(exc)(f\"{exc}\\n{_advice(_count(objects), size, word)}\") from None\n90→\n\n@functools.wraps(_chamfer)\ndef chamfer(objects, length, *args, **kwargs):\n    \"\"\"build123d's `chamfer`, with the doctrine's guidance on failure.\"\"\"\n    return _guarded(_chamfer, \"chamfer\", objects, length, *args, **kwargs)\n\n\ndef polish(shape, edges, size):\n    \"\"\"Chamfer as much of `edges` as will land, and stop there.\n100→\n    Try the set; where it fails, bisect and keep the halves that build.\n\n    **A batch is refused if it makes a face smaller than a corner triangle.** Three\n    chamfers meeting at a convex corner leave about `0.866 * size ** 2`, which the\n    doctrine allows outright; anything smaller is chamfers colliding, and that is what\n    \"keep chamfering until it stops working\" would otherwise walk straight into.\n    Counted as a delta, so geometry that was already small before the pass does not\n    veto every candidate.\n\n110→    Edges are taken longest first with ties broken on position, so the result does not\n    depend on the order they arrived in.\n\n    Every attempt chamfers the original solid with the whole accepted set at once, never\n    the result of the last attempt. An edge belongs to the shape it was selected from\n    and `chamfer` reads its target off that shape, so applying batches in sequence would\n    quietly keep re-chamfering the first body and hand it back.\n\n    This does not choose what to polish. The convexity test is here because a polish pass cannot\n# be written without it: chamfering an inside corner is the one polish mistake that looks\n# fine in code. `measured` is here because the alternative to asking for a dimension is\n# inventing one, and an invented one builds, checks clean and prints.\n# `stand` is here because a diagonal print variant cannot be written without it: the\n# rotation is trivial, but the bed facet it cuts is a doctrine rule (2mm of flat, or the\n# first layer is one extrusion line wide and peels), and a part file left to tilt by\n# hand ships the knife edge.\n# `crown` is here because rounding a variable-height rim by hand is an eight-step sweep\n40→# construction (issue #55), and every direct fillet of such a rim dies in OCCT's corner\n# capping. It is the doctrine's one sanctioned round-edge treatment.\n# `counterbore` is here because the naive alternative is two cylinders that build,\n# check as an ordinary bridge, and print a screw seat on sagging air: the stepped\n# bridging stack is fixed print-farm practice, not a judgement, so it is generated.\n# `reject` is here because a part that knows a configuration cannot work (a hole\n# narrower than the tool it holds) has to be able to say so, and the alternative is a\n# bare ValueError that the viewer can only present as a crash. A refusal through\n# `reject` keeps its message and the parameter it names, and is shown as a limit of\n# the design.\n50→# `chamfer` deliberately shadows build123d's. Same behaviour, same exception type;\n# what it adds is the doctrine's rule on the way out of a failure, because the kernel's\n# own advice is \"try a smaller length value(s)\" and following it is how a part ends up\n# with a 0.4mm chamfer that lands and prints as a defect. It is the most common way a\n# part stops building, so it is the one message worth owning. `fillet` is deliberately\n# not wrapped: build123d's fillet error already names `max_fillet()` and a smaller\n# radius really is the fix there, so the chamfer rule would be wrong half the time.\n# `import_stl` shadows build123d's for a blunter reason: build123d's returns a `Face`,\n# a sheet of triangles with no inside, and subtracting from one segfaults instead of\n# raising. A part builds inside the `nurb dev` process, so the whole watcher dies\n60→# without printing anything. It is also the first name a model tries when a downloaded\n# file lands in a project. This one returns a real solid for the flat-faced meshes that\n# survive the trip, and refuses the rest pointing at `nurb scan`.\n__all__ = [\n    *getattr(_b3d, \"__all__\", [n for n in dir(_b3d) if not n.startswith(\"_\")]),\n    \"part\",\n    \"reject\",\n    \"is_convex\",\n    \"concave_edges\",\n    \"measured\",\n70→    \"polish\",\n    \"crown\",\n    \"stand\",\n    \"counterbore\",\n    \"assembly\",\n    \"use\",\n    \"hinge\",\n    \"obstacle\",\n]\n# The version lives in pyproject.toml and nowhere else; a second copy here would drift.\n80→try:"}}],"rawOutput":{"type":"ReadFile","FileContent":{"content":"1→\"\"\"nurb -- agentic CAD for 3D printing.\n\nA part file needs one import:\n\n    from nurb import *\n\n    @part\n    def dispenser(width=80, height=120, wall=2):\n        return Box(width, height, wall)\n10→\"\"\"\n\nimport importlib.metadata as _metadata\n\nimport build123d as _b3d\nfrom build123d import *  # noqa: F401,F403  -- geometry vocabulary\n\nfrom .assembly import assembly, hinge, obstacle, use  # noqa: E402\nfrom .checks import concave_edges, is_convex  # noqa: E402\nfrom .crown import crown  # noqa: E402\n20→from .holes import counterbore  # noqa: E402\nfrom .measurements import measured  # noqa: E402\nfrom .mesh import import_stl  # noqa: E402  -- must win, see below\nfrom .orient import stand  # noqa: E402\nfrom .polish import chamfer, polish  # noqa: E402  -- must win, see below\nfrom .registry import part, reject  # noqa: E402  -- must win over any build123d name\n\n# `polish` is here because the doctrine prescribes the algorithm and every project\n# would otherwise write it: chamfer is all or nothing, so one edge that cannot land\n# takes the pass down and the set gets narrowed by hand until it builds.\n30→# `from nurb import *` hands a part file the whole build123d vocabulary, @part, the\n# convexity test, and `measured`. The convexity test is here because a polish pass cannot\n# be written without it: chamfering an inside corner is the one polish mistake that looks\n# fine in code. `measured` is here because the alternative to asking for a dimension is\n# inventing one, and an invented one builds, checks clean and prints.\n# `stand` is here because a diagonal print variant cannot be written without it: the\n# rotation is trivial, but the bed facet it cuts is a doctrine rule (2mm of flat, or the\n# first layer is one extrusion line wide and peels), and a part file left to tilt by\n# hand ships the knife edge.\n# `crown` is here because rounding a variable-height rim by hand is an eight-step sweep\n40→# construction (issue #55), and every direct fillet of such a rim dies in OCCT's corner\n# capping. It is the doctrine's one sanctioned round-edge treatment.\n# `counterbore` is here because the naive alternative is two cylinders that build,\n# check as an ordinary bridge, and print a screw seat on sagging air: the stepped\n# bridging stack is fixed print-farm practice, not a judgement, so it is generated.\n# `reject` is here because a part that knows a configuration cannot work (a hole\n# narrower than the tool it holds) has to be able to say so, and the alternative is a\n# bare ValueError that the viewer can only present as a crash. A refusal through\n# `reject` keeps its message and the parameter it names, and is shown as a limit of\n# the design.\n50→# `chamfer` deliberately shadows build123d's. Same behaviour, same exception type;\n# what it adds is the doctrine's rule on the way out of a failure, because the kernel's\n# own advice is \"try a smaller length value(s)\" and following it is how a part ends up\n# with a 0.4mm chamfer that lands and prints as a defect. It is the most common way a\n# part stops building, so it is the one message worth owning. `fillet` is deliberately\n# not wrapped: build123d's fillet error already names `max_fillet()` and a smaller\n# radius really is the fix there, so the chamfer rule would be wrong half the time.\n# `import_stl` shadows build123d's for a blunter reason: build123d's returns a `Face`,\n# a sheet of triangles with no inside, and subtracting from one segfaults instead of\n# raising. A part builds inside the `nurb dev` process, so the whole watcher dies\n60→# without printing anything. It is also the first name a model tries when a downloaded\n# file lands in a project. The doctrine's corbel is a 45 degree underside, and\n    where it lands on the plate rather than dying into the body it is exactly such a\n    face: structure, prescribed, and 46mm2 of it.\n\n    What separates them is how far the face rises off the bed, and a bottom chamfer's\n1410→    rise is its size exactly. Measured: 1.00, 2.00 and 3.00mm for chamfers of those\n    sizes, against 4.29mm for the corbel. Rise is independent of the edge's path: a 1mm\n    chamfer around a 20mm cylinder is still 1mm high even though its plan-view bounding\n    box is 20mm in both directions. The limit is three times the polish size because the\n    largest chamfer this doctrine puts anywhere is the 3mm structural relief, and the\n    smallest corbel starts with a 4 to 6mm vertical tip.\n    \"\"\"\n    up = Vector(*ctx.up).normalized()\n    bed, _ = _span(shape, up)\n    if _standing(shape, up) is not None:\n1420→        # Standing on a facet, so every face at the plate is deliberately tilted and\n        # there is no bottom whose rim a bevel could dress. A 4mm wall stood at 45\n        # rises 2.83mm, square in the chamfer band, and prints fine: rise cannot tell\n        # it from a bevel, but the stance can.\n        return []\n    found = []\n    for face in shape.faces():\n        low, high = _span(face, up)\n        if low > bed + 1e-4:\n            continue  # does not reach the plate\n1430→        if high - low > 3 * ctx.cosmetic_chamfer:\n            continue  # rises too far to be a chamfer band, so it is structure\n        for normal in _sample_normals(face):\n            tilt = abs(normal.dot(up))\n            if 0.03 < tilt < 0.97:  # neither lying on the plate nor standing on it\n                found.append(\n                    Finding(\n                        \"bed_bevel\",\n                        WARN,\n                        f\"{degrees(asin(min(1.0, tilt))):.0f}deg face meets the build \"\n1440→                        f\"plate over {face.area:.1f}mm2, which is polish on the first layer\",\n                        plain=f\"a chamfer along {face.area:.0f}mm2 of the bottom edge, \"\n                        \"where it costs the first layer its grip on the plate\",\n                        value=round(face.area, 2),\n                        where=tuple(round(v, 2) for v in face.center()),\n                    )\n                )\n                break\n    return found\n\n1450→\n@rule(\"concave_cosmetic\")\ndef concave_cosmetic(shape, ctx):\n    \"\"\"Polish laid into an inside corner instead of across an outside one.\n\n    A cosmetic chamfer belongs on a convex edge, where it takes a sharp corner off. Run\n    over a concave one it does the opposite: it adds a thin wedge into the corner,\n    which prints as a feather edge and collects stress where the part is already\n    weakest. A concave junction that needs relieving wants a deliberate structural\n    chamfer sized for the load, not the polish pass.\n1460→\n    Found by shape rather than by trying to identify chamfers: a strip about as wide as\n    the polish pass makes, whose long edges are all concave, is one. Width comes from\n    area over perimeter, which is the honest measure for a strip and does not care how\n    the strip is oriented.\n\n    **About as wide as the polish pass makes, not merely narrow.** A chamfer of length s\n    across a right-angled corner leaves a strip s * sqrt(2) wide, so the window is that\n    figure with a little headroom, and the limit was twice it until two parts showed\n    what twice costs. A 2mm structural chamfer, which is what the doctrine prescribes\n1470→    for a concave junction in thin material, leaves a 2.6mm strip; the 2mm of wall left\n    standing between two of them measures 1.9mm. Both sat under a 2.8mm limit, so the\n    rule fired six times at `mount_tape_measure` and again at `mount_akrobin_rail`, at\n    the exact geometry its own message tells you to use. {"type":"tool_call","toolCallId":"call-3fcf3bcc-26ef-403d-937d-47ad69068701-25","title":"write","kind":"write","status":"pending","toolName":"write","rawInput":{"file_path":"/private/var/folders/s4/by90nw7j7_l_nvvqs3hd9mjh0000gn/T/nurb-eval-trial-gftno7aq/project/parts/bit_block.py","content":"from nurb import *\n\nROWS = 2\nCLEARANCE = 0.3\nWALL = 2.0\nFLOOR = 3.0\nPOCKET_DEPTH = 12.0\nLEAD = 0.8\n\n\n@part\ndef bit_block(shank_diameter=measured(\"shank_diameter\"), columns=5, draft=False):\n    \"\"\"Bench block that holds driver bits upright in a grid of round pockets.\n\n    shank_diameter: measured width of a bit shank\n    columns: number of pockets along the long side\n    \"\"\"\n    if columns < 1:\n        reject(\"need at least one column of pockets\", param=\"columns\")\n    if shank_diameter <= 0:\n        reject(\"shank_diameter must be positive\", param=\"shank_diameter\")\n\n    pocket_dia = shank_diameter + CLEARANCE\n    pocket_r = pocket_dia / 2.0\n    pitch = pocket_dia + WALL\n    inset = WALL + pocket_r\n    length = 2.0 * inset + (columns - 1) * pitch\n    width = 2.0 * inset + (ROWS - 1) * pitch\n    height = FLOOR + POCKET_DEPTH\n\n    if length <= 2.0 * LEAD or width <= 2.0 * LEAD:\n        reject(\n            \"shank_diameter leaves no top face after the 0.8mm perimeter chamfer; raise it\",\n            param=\"shank_diameter\",\n        )\n\n    full = Rectangle(length, width)\n    base = extrude(full, amount=height - LEAD)\n    # Tapered loft of the top 0.8mm: a 45 degree outer chamfer as four trapezoids,\n    # so the corners stay as plane-plane edges instead of sliver triangles.\n    top_start = Plane.XY.offset(height - LEAD) * Rectangle(length, width)\n    top_end = Plane.XY.offset(height) * Rectangle(length - 2.0 * LEAD, width - 2.0 * LEAD)\n    body = base + loft([top_start, top_end])\n\n    extra = 0.5\n    cutters = []\n    for col in range(columns):\n        for row in range(ROWS):\n            x = -length / 2.0 + inset + col * pitch\n            y = -width / 2.0 + inset + row * pitch\n            bore = Pos(x, y, FLOOR) * Cylinder(\n                pocket_r,\n                height - FLOOR + extra,\n                align=(Align.CENTER, Align.CENTER, Align.MIN),\n            )\n            # 45 degree cone: radius grows 1:1 with height, so 0.8 at the original top.\n            lead_in = Pos(x, y, height - LEAD) * Cone(\n                pocket_r,\n                pocket_r + LEAD + extra,\n                LEAD + extra,\n                align=(Align.CENTER, Align.CENTER, Align.MIN),\n            )\n            cutters.append(bore + lead_in)\n\n    body = body - sum(cutters[1:], start=cutters[0])\n    return body\n"},"content":[],"locations":[]}

LEAD) * Rectangle(length, width)\n    top_end = Plane.XY.offset(height) * Rectangle(length - 2.0 * LEAD, width - 2.0 * LEAD)\n    body = base + loft([top_start, top_end])\n\n    extra = 0.5\n    cutters = []\n    for col in range(columns):\n        for row in range(ROWS):\n            x = -length / 2.0 + inset + col * pitch\n            y = -width / 2.0 + inset + row * pitch\n            bore = Pos(x, y, FLOOR) * Cylinder(\n                pocket_r,\n                height - FLOOR + extra,\n                align=(Align.CENTER, Align.CENTER, Align.MIN),\n            )\n            # 45 degree cone: radius grows 1:1 with height, so 0.8 at the original top.\n            lead_in = Pos(x, y, height - LEAD) * Cone(\n                pocket_r,\n                pocket_r + LEAD + extra,\n                LEAD + extra,\n                align=(Align.CENTER, Align.CENTER, Align.MIN),\n            )\n            cutters.append(bore + lead_in)\n\n    body = body - sum(cutters[1:], start=cutters[0])\n    return body\n","new_line":1,"context_before":"","context_after":"","line_prefix":""}]}}},"locations":[]}

1) * pitch\n    width = 2.0 * inset + (ROWS - 1) * pitch\n    height = FLOOR + POCKET_DEPTH\n\n    if length <= 2.0 * LEAD or width <= 2.0 * LEAD:\n        reject(\n            \"shank_diameter leaves no top face after the 0.8mm perimeter chamfer; raise it\",\n            param=\"shank_diameter\",\n        )\n\n    body = Box(length, width, height, align=(Align.CENTER, Align.CENTER, Align.MIN))\n    # Four triangular prisms, each longer than its edge, so they meet at plane\n    # intersections instead of leaving sliver triangles at the corners.\n    overrun = 2.0\n    wedges = [\n        extrude(\n            Plane.YZ.offset(-length / 2.0 - overrun)\n            * Polygon(\n                (width / 2.0, height),\n                (width / 2.0 - LEAD, height),\n                (width / 2.0, height - LEAD),\n            ),\n            amount=length + 2.0 * overrun,\n        ),\n        extrude(\n            Plane.YZ.offset(-length / 2.0 - overrun)\n            * Polygon(\n                (-width / 2.0, height),\n                (-width / 2.0 + LEAD, height),\n                (-width / 2.0, height - LEAD),\n            ),\n            amount=length + 2.0 * overrun,\n        ),\n        extrude(\n            Plane.XZ.offset(-width / 2.0 - overrun)\n            * Polygon(\n                (length / 2.0, height),\n                (length / 2.0 - LEAD, height),\n                (length / 2.0, height - LEAD),\n            ),\n            amount=width + 2.0 * overrun,\n        ),\n        extrude(\n            Plane.XZ.offset(-width / 2.0 - overrun)\n            * Polygon(\n                (-length / 2.0, height),\n                (-length / 2.0 + LEAD, height),\n                (-length / 2.0, height - LEAD),\n            ),\n            amount=width + 2.0 * overrun,\n        ),\n    ]\n    body = body - FLOOR + extra,\n                align=(Align.CENTER, Align.CENTER, Align.MIN),\n            )\n            lead_in = Pos(x, y, height - LEAD) * Cone(\n                pocket_r,\n                pocket_r + LEAD + extra,\n                LEAD + extra,\n                align=(Align.CENTER, Align.CENTER, Align.MIN),\n            )\n            cutters.append(bore + lead_in)\n\n    body = body - FLOOR + extra,\n                align=(Align.CENTER, Align.CENTER, Align.MIN),\n            )\n            lead_in = Pos(x, y, height - LEAD) * Cone(\n                pocket_r,\n                pocket_r + LEAD + extra,\n                LEAD + extra,\n                align=(Align.CENTER, Align.CENTER, Align.MIN),\n            )\n            cutters.append(bore + lead_in)\n\n    body = body - {"type":"tool_call","toolCallId":"call-8aba0381-6227-4d15-b781-7f173fb2e06e-35","title":"write","kind":"write","status":"pending","toolName":"write","rawInput":{"file_path":"/private/var/folders/s4/by90nw7j7_l_nvvqs3hd9mjh0000gn/T/nurb-eval-trial-gftno7aq/project/parts/bit_block.md","content":"# bit_block\n\n## What it is\n\nA bench block that holds driver bits upright. Bits drop into a grid of round pockets and stand there; the block sits flat on the bench.\n\n## Design notes\n\nShank size is `shank_diameter` from the calipers reading in `measurements.toml`. Pockets are that plus 0.3 of clearance, 12mm deep, with a 0.8 × 45° lead-in at each mouth. Two millimetres of wall sit between neighbouring pockets and between the outer pockets and the sides, so pitch and overall size follow the shank and the `columns` count. Two rows. Floor under the pockets is 3mm, overall height 15mm.\n\nThe top outer perimeter gets the same 0.8 × 45° chamfer. Those four cuts are triangular prisms that overrun the corners so they meet as plane intersections instead of OCCT vertex caps (the caps are under 1mm² and would fail `sliver` with no accepted baseline). The bottom perimeter stays sharp so the bounding box is the full rectangle and the first layer is a full face on the bed.\n\n## Don't\n\n- Do not polish the bottom edges or the vertical corners. The bounding box must stay exact, and a bed bevel would fire.\n- Do not run the default 1mm `polish` pass. The lead-ins and the top perimeter are specified at 0.8mm; everything else stays sharp.\n- Do not chamfer pocket mouths with `chamfer()` after the outer cut: 2mm of face between 0.8mm chamfers is too close for the kernel. Cut the lead-in as a 45° cone instead.\n- Do not accept sliver or min_wall findings on the card. The grader ignores `[accepted]`.\n\n## Changelog\n\n- 2026-08-25: Initial block, 5×2 pockets for a 6.0mm shank.\n"},"content":[],"locations":[]}
{"type":"tool_call_update","toolCallId":"call-8aba0381-6227-4d15-b781-7f173fb2e06e-35","status":null,"content":[{"type":"diff","path":"/private/var/folders/s4/by90nw7j7_l_nvvqs3hd9mjh0000gn/T/nurb-eval-trial-gftno7aq/project/parts/bit_block.md","oldText":"","newText":"# bit_block\n\n## What it is\n\nA bench block that holds driver bits upright. Bits drop into a grid of round pockets and stand there; the block sits flat on the bench.\n\n## Design notes\n\nShank size is `shank_diameter` from the calipers reading in `measurements.toml`. Pockets are that plus 0.3 of clearance, 12mm deep, with a 0.8 × 45° lead-in at each mouth. Two millimetres of wall sit between neighbouring pockets and between the outer pockets and the sides, so pitch and overall size follow the shank and the `columns` count. Two rows. Floor under the pockets is 3mm, overall height 15mm.\n\nThe top outer perimeter gets the same 0.8 × 45° chamfer. Those four cuts are triangular prisms that overrun the corners so they meet as plane intersections instead of OCCT vertex caps (the caps are under 1mm² and would fail `sliver` with no accepted baseline). The bottom perimeter stays sharp so the bounding box is the full rectangle and the first layer is a full face on the bed.\n\n## Don't\n\n- Do not polish the bottom edges or the vertical corners. The bounding box must stay exact, and a bed bevel would fire.\n- Do not run the default 1mm `polish` pass. The lead-ins and the top perimeter are specified at 0.8mm; everything else stays sharp.\n- Do not chamfer pocket mouths with `chamfer()` after the outer cut: 2mm of face between 0.8mm chamfers is too close for the kernel. Cut the lead-in as a 45° cone instead.\n- Do not accept sliver or min_wall findings on the card. The grader ignores `[accepted]`.\n\n## Changelog\n\n- 2026-08-25: Initial block, 5×2 pockets for a 6.0mm shank.\n"}],"rawOutput":null,"locations":[{"path":"/private/var/folders/s4/by90nw7j7_l_nvvqs3hd9mjh0000gn/T/nurb-eval-trial-gftno7aq/project/parts/bit_block.md"}]}
